The vertex is exactly halfway between the focus and the directrix. Since the vertex is at (0, 0) and the focus is at (0, 4), I can figure out where the directrix must be.

If the focus is 4 units above the vertex along the y-axis, then the directrix should be 4 units below the vertex to maintain symmetry. So that would place the directrix at y = -4. 

Now, for a parabola opening upwards or downwards, the standard form of the equation is given by \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \). But since the vertex is at (0, 0), this simplifies things because the vertex form of a parabola is \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \) where (h, k) is the vertex. In this case, h and k are both 0, so it reduces to \( y = ax^2 \).

I need to find the value of 'a'. I recall that for a parabola, the distance from the vertex to the focus is denoted by p. The formula relating 'a' to p is \( a = \frac{1}{4p} \). 

Given that the focus is at (0, 4), the distance p from the vertex (which is at (0, 0)) to the focus is 4 units. So plugging this into the equation:

\( a = \frac{1}{4 \times 4} = \frac{1}{16} \).

So substituting back into the vertex form, the equation of the parabola should be \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

\( \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 4)^2} \)

And the distance to the directrix is simply |y - (-4)| = |y + 4|.

\( x^2 + (y - 4)^2 = (y + 4)^2 \)

Expanding both sides:

Left side: \( x^2 + y^2 - 8y + 16 \)
Right side: \( y^2 + 8y + 16 \)

Subtracting the right side from both sides:

\( x^2 + y^2 - 8y + 16 - y^2 - 8y - 16 = 0 \)

Simplifying this, the y squared and constants cancel out:

\( x^2 - 16y = 0 \)

Which leads to \( x^2 = 16y \), so solving for y gives \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \). 

Yes, that matches my earlier result. So I think this is correct.

First, I recognize that the standard form of a parabola's equation is \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), where \((h, k)\) is the vertex. Given the vertex at (5, -2), I substitute these values into the equation to get \( y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2 \).

Next, since the parabola opens downward, the coefficient \(a\) must be negative.

The focus of the parabola is given as (5, -4). For a parabola in this form, the distance from the vertex to the focus is determined by \(1/(4|a|)\). Therefore, I calculate this distance using the y-coordinates: |-2 - (-4)| = 2.

Setting up the equation \( |1/(4|a|) = 2 \), I solve for \(a\):
\( 1/(4|a|) = 2 \)
\( 4|a| = 1/2 \)
\( |a| = 1/8 \)

Since the parabola opens downward, \( a = -1/8 \).

Finally, substituting \( a \) back into the equation gives:
\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 5)^2 - 2 \).

**Solution:**

To find the equation of a parabola that opens downwards with a vertex at (5, -2) and a focus at (5, -4), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Vertex Form of the Parabola:**
   
   The standard form of a parabola's equation is:
   \[
   y = a(x - h)^2 + k
   \]
   where \((h, k)\) is the vertex.

   Given the vertex at (5, -2), substitute \(h = 5\) and \(k = -2\):
   \[
   y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2
   \]

2. **Determine the Value of \(a\):**
   
   The distance between the vertex and the focus is given by:
   \[
   |1/(4|a|)| = \text{Distance from Vertex to Focus}
   \]
   
   Here, the focus is at (5, -4). Calculate the vertical distance from the vertex (y-coordinate of the vertex to y-coordinate of the focus):
   \[
   |-2 - (-4)| = 2
   \]
   
   Set this equal to \(1/(4|a|)\) and solve for \(|a|\):
   \[
   |1/(4|a|)| = 2 \\
   \Rightarrow 1/(4|a|) = 2 \\
   \Rightarrow 4|a| = 1/2 \\
   \Rightarrow |a| = \frac{1}{8}
   \]
   
   Since the parabola opens downward, \(a\) is negative:
   \[
   a = -\frac{1}{8}
   \]

3. **Write the Final Equation:**
   
   Substitute \(a = -\frac{1}{8}\) back into the vertex form equation:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 5)^2 - 2
   \]
   
   This is the equation of the parabola in standard form.

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: } y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 5)^2 - 2}
\]

The vertex form of the parabola given by \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) is found by completing the square. 

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \):**
   Start with the equation:
   \[
   y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]
   Factor out 3 from the first two terms:
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

2. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   To complete the square for \( x^2 + 2x \):
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \).
   - Square it to get \( 1^2 = 1 \).

3. **Add and subtract this square inside the equation:**
   Since we added an implicit 1 inside the parentheses (because it's multiplied by 3), we need to balance the equation by subtracting the same value:
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   y = 3((x + 1)^2 - 1) + 5
   \]

4. **Distribute the 3 and combine constants:**
   Multiply 3 through the parentheses:
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

The vertex form of the parabola is:
\[
y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]

**Final Answer:**
The vertex form is \( y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \).

\( 8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12 \)

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{4}{8}x - \frac{12}{8} \)

Simplifying the fractions:

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2} \)

Now, I'll focus on completing the square for the quadratic expression in terms of x. I'll factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms.

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2} \)

To complete the square inside the parentheses, I'll take half of the coefficient of x, which is -4. Half of that is -2, and squaring it gives 4.

I'll add and subtract this value inside the equation:

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4) - \frac{3}{2} \)

This can be rewritten as:

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}((x - 2)^2 - 4) - \frac{3}{2} \)

Now, I'll distribute the coefficient outside the parentheses:

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2} \)

Finally, I'll combine the constant terms:

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \)

So, the vertex form of the equation is \( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \).
</think>

To convert the standard form equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) to the vertex form of a parabola, follow these steps:

**Step 1: Isolate the y-term**

Start by moving all terms except those involving y to the right side of the equation.

\[
x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0
\]
\[
8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12
\]

**Step 2: Solve for y**

Divide both sides by 8 to solve for y:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 3: Complete the Square**

To complete the square, focus on the quadratic and linear terms of x:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -2 \), and square it to get 4. Add and subtract this value inside the equation:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left( (x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 \right) - \frac{3}{2}
\]
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left( (x - 2)^2 - 4 \right) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 4: Distribute and Simplify**

Distribute the \( -\frac{1}{8} \):

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2}
\]
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

**Final Answer:**

The vertex form of the equation is:

\[
\boxed{\ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 }
\]

The quadratic equation passing through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4) is found by solving for the coefficients a, b, and c in y = ax² + bx + c.

After substituting each point into the general form of the parabola's equation, we set up the following system:
- From (0, 4): c = 4
- From (2, 8): 4a + 2b = 4
- From (-1, 3): a - b = -1

Solving this system yields:
a = 1/3,
b = 4/3,
c = 4.

Thus, the equation of the parabola is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

To find the equation of the circle, I'll start by determining its center. The center is the midpoint between the two endpoints of the diameter.

First, I'll calculate the average of the x-coordinates and the y-coordinates separately to get the coordinates of the center.

Next, I'll use the distance formula to find the length of the radius, which is half the diameter's length.

With both the center and the radius known, I can plug these values into the standard equation of a circle.
</think>

To determine the equation of the circle with endpoints of its diameter at \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Find the Center (Midpoint)**
The center \((h, k)\) of the circle is the midpoint of the diameter. The midpoint formula is:
\[
(h, k) = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2 \\
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center is \((2, -1)\).

### **Step 2: Find the Radius**
The radius \(r\) is half the length of the diameter. First, find the distance between the two endpoints using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} \\
= \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} \\
= \sqrt{64 + 16} \\
= \sqrt{80} \\
= 4\sqrt{5}
\]
Therefore, the radius is:
\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

### **Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle**
The standard form of the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting the found values:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2 \\
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

### **Final Answer**
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20}
\]

**Step 1: Group like terms**

\[
(x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) - 11 = 0
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square**

For the \( x \)-terms:
- Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \)
- Square it: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \)
- Add and subtract this value inside the equation.

For the \( y \)-terms:
- Take half of the coefficient of \( y \): \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \)
- Square it: \( (1)^2 = 1 \)
- Add and subtract this value inside the equation.

\[

\]

**Step 3: Simplify**

\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Substitute the linear equation into the circle's equation:**  
   Replace \( y \) in \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \) with \( x + 3 \):
   \[
   x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
   \]

2. **Expand and simplify the equation:**  
   Expand \( (x + 3)^2 \):
   \[
   x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
   \]
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   2x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
   \]

3. **Rearrange to form a quadratic equation:**  
   Subtract 17 from both sides:
   \[
   2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
   \]
   Divide the entire equation by 2 for simplification:
   \[
   x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
   \]

4. **Factor the quadratic equation:**  
   Find two numbers that multiply to -4 and add to 3, which are 4 and -1:
   \[
   (x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
   \]
   
5. **Solve for x-values:**  
   Set each factor equal to zero:
   \[
   x + 4 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -4 \\
   x - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 1
   \]

6. **Find corresponding y-values using \( y = x + 3 \):**  
   For \( x = -4 \):
   \[
   y = -4 + 3 = -1
   \]
   So, one point is (-4, -1).

   For \( x = 1 \):
   \[
   y = 1 + 3 = 4
   \]
   The other point is (1, 4).

7. **Verify the points in the circle equation:**  
   Both points satisfy \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), confirming their correctness.

First, I recognize that to find the equation of a circle passing through three given points, I can use the general equation of a circle:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

Next, I'll substitute each of the three points into this equation to create a system of equations. Using the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1):

1. For the point \((1, 1)\):
\[
(1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Expanding this gives:
\[
h^2 - 2h + 1 + k^2 - 2k + 1 = r^2 \\
h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2 = r^2 \quad (1)
\]

2. For the point \((2, -3)\):
\[
(2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Expanding this gives:
\[
h^2 - 4h + 4 + k^2 + 6k + 9 = r^2 \\
h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13 = r^2 \quad (2)
\]

3. For the point \((5, -1)\):
\[
(5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Expanding this gives:
\[
h^2 - 10h + 25 + k^2 + 2k + 1 = r^2 \\
h^2 + k^2 - 10h + 2k + 26 = r^2 \quad (3)
\]

Now, I'll subtract equation (1) from equation (2) to eliminate \(r^2\) and obtain an equation involving only \(h\) and \(k\):
\[
(h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13) - (h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2) = 0 \\
-2h + 8k + 11 = 0
\]
Simplifying this gives:
\[
-2h + 8k = -11 \quad (4)
\]

Next, I'll subtract equation (1) from equation (3):
\[
(h^2 + k^2 - 10h + 2k + 26) - (h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2) = 0 \\
-8h + 4k + 24 = 0
\]
Simplifying this gives:
\[
-8h + 4k = -24 \quad (5)
\]

Now, I have two equations:
1. \( -2h + 8k = -11 \) (Equation 4)
2. \( -8h + 4k = -24 \) (Equation 5)

I'll solve these simultaneously. Multiplying Equation 4 by 2 to align the coefficients of \(k\):
\[
-4h + 16k = -22 \quad (6)
\]

Now, subtracting Equation 5 from Equation 6:
\[
(-4h + 16k) - (-8h + 4k) = -22 - (-24) \\
4h + 12k = 2
\]
Simplifying this gives:
\[
h + 3k = \frac{1}{2} \quad (7)
\]

From Equation 7, I can express \(h\) in terms of \(k\):
\[
h = \frac{1}{2} - 3k
\]

Substituting this into Equation 4:
\[
-2\left(\frac{1}{2} - 3k\right) + 8k = -11 \\
-1 + 6k + 8k = -11 \\
14k = -10 \\
k = -\frac{5}{7}
\]

Now, substituting \(k\) back into Equation 7 to find \(h\):
\[
h = \frac{1}{2} - 3\left(-\frac{5}{7}\right) \\
h = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{15}{7} \\
h = \frac{7}{14} + \frac{30}{14} \\
h = \frac{37}{14}
\]

Finally, substituting \(h\) and \(k\) back into Equation (1) to find \(r^2\):
\[
\left(\frac{37}{14}\right)^2 + \left(-\frac{5}{7}\right)^2 - 2\left(\frac{37}{14}\right) - 2\left(-\frac{5}{7}\right) + 2 = r^2 \\
\]
After calculating, I find:
\[
r^2 = \frac{620}{98} = \frac{310}{49}
\]

Therefore, the equation of the circle is:
\[
\left(x - \frac{37}{14}\right)^2 + \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \frac{310}{49}
\]
</think>

To determine the equation of a circle passing through three given points, we follow these steps:

**Step 1: Use the General Equation of a Circle**

The general equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 2: Substitute Each Point into the General Equation**

Given points \((1, 1)\), \((2, -3)\), and \((5, -1)\), we substitute each into the general equation to form a system of equations:

1. For \((1, 1)\):
\[
(1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2 \\
h^2 - 2h + 1 + k^2 - 2k + 1 = r^2 \\
h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2 = r^2 \quad (1)
\]

2. For \((2, -3)\):
\[
(2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2 \\
h^2 - 4h + 4 + k^2 + 6k + 9 = r^2 \\
h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13 = r^2 \quad (2)
\]

3. For \((5, -1)\):
\[
(5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2 \\
h^2 - 10h + 25 + k^2 + 2k + 1 = r^2 \\
h^2 + k^2 - 10h + 2k + 26 = r^2 \quad (3)
\]

**Step 3: Subtract Equation (1) from Equations (2) and (3)**

Subtracting equation (1) from equation (2):
\[
(h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13) - (h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2) = 0 \\
-2h + 8k + 11 = 0 \quad (4)
\]

Subtracting equation (1) from equation (3):
\[
(h^2 + k^2 - 10h + 2k + 26) - (h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2) = 0 \\
-8h + 4k + 24 = 0 \quad (5)
\]

**Step 4: Solve the System of Equations**

From equations (4) and (5):
1. \( -2h + 8k = -11 \) (Equation 4)
2. \( -8h + 4k = -24 \) (Equation 5)

Multiply equation (4) by 2 to align the coefficients of \(k\):
\[
-4h + 16k = -22 \quad (6)
\]

Subtracting equation (5) from equation (6):
\[
(-4h + 16k) - (-8h + 4k) = -22 - (-24) \\
4h + 12k = 2 \\
h + 3k = \frac{1}{2} \quad (7)
\]

From equation (7):
\[
h = \frac{1}{2} - 3k
\]

Substitute \( h \) back into equation (4):
\[
-2\left(\frac{1}{2} - 3k\right) + 8k = -11 \\
-1 + 6k + 8k = -11 \\
14k = -10 \\
k = -\frac{5}{7}
\]

Now, substitute \( k \) back into equation (7):
\[
h = \frac{1}{2} - 3\left(-\frac{5}{7}\right) \\
h = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{15}{7} \\
h = \frac{7}{14} + \frac{30}{14} \\
h = \frac{37}{14}
\]

**Step 5: Find \( r^2 \)**

Substitute \( h \) and \( k \) back into equation (1):
\[
\left(\frac{37}{14}\right)^2 + \left(-\frac{5}{7}\right)^2 - 2\left(\frac{37}{14}\right) - 2\left(-\frac{5}{7}\right) + 2 = r^2 \\
\]
After calculating:
\[
r^2 = \frac{620}{98} = \frac{310}{49}
\]

**Final Equation of the Circle**

\[
\boxed{\left(x - \frac{37}{14}\right)^2 + \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \frac{310}{49}}
\]

The given circle equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \) can be rewritten by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \). 

1. **Group terms**: 
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) + 20 = 0
   \]

2. **Complete the square**:
   - For \( x^2 - 8x \), add and subtract \( (-4)^2 = 16 \):
     \[
     x^2 - 8x + 16 = (x - 4)^2
     \]
   - For \( y^2 - 6y \), add and subtract \( (-3)^2 = 9 \):
     \[
     y^2 - 6y + 9 = (y - 3)^2
     \]

3. **Adjust the equation**:
   Add these squares to both sides:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 + 20 = 0
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 + 5 = 0 \implies (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

The equation is now in standard form, revealing the center \( (4, 3) \) and radius \( \sqrt{5} \).

**Final Answer**: Center: {4, 3}, Radius: √5

The general equation for an ellipse with its major axis along the x-axis is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a\) represents the semi-major axis and \(b\) represents the semi-minor axis.

Given that the length of the horizontal major axis is 10, the semi-major axis \(a\) is half of that, so \(a = 5\).

To find the equation of an ellipse with a center at (0, 0), a horizontal major axis of length 10, and a minor axis of length 6, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Recall the Standard Form of an Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin (0, 0) with its major axis along the x-axis is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where:
- \( a \) is the semi-major axis length.
- \( b \) is the semi-minor axis length.

### Step 2: Determine the Semi-Major and Semi-Minor Axes
Given:
- Length of the horizontal major axis (which is \( 2a \)) = 10. Therefore,
\[
a = \frac{10}{2} = 5
\]
- Length of the minor axis (which is \( 2b \)) = 6. Therefore,
\[
b = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

### Step 3: Substitute the Values into the Standard Form
Plugging \( a^2 = 25 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \) into the equation:
\[
\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25} + \dfrac{y^2}{9} = 1}
\]

The standard form of an ellipse is usually written as (x-h)²/a² + (y-k)²/b² = 1, where (h,k) is the center.

That makes sense because the major axis is along the x-axis since the denominator under x² is larger.

Now, eccentricity (e) of an ellipse measures how "stretched" it is. The formula for eccentricity depends on whether a > b or b > a. Since in this case a = 6 and b = 3, which are both positive real numbers with a greater than b, the major axis is along the x-axis.

The formula for the eccentricity of an ellipse when a > b is e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to a focus. I remember that in an ellipse, c² = a² - b². So first, I need to compute c by taking the square root of (a² - b²).

3. Subtract them: 36 - 9 = 27.
4. Take the square root of that result: sqrt(27). Wait, what's sqrt(27)? Hmm, I know that 27 is 9*3, so sqrt(9*3) = sqrt(9)*sqrt(3) = 3√3.

So c equals 3√3. Now, since e = c/a, substitute the values:

e = (3√3)/6

Simplify this fraction: both numerator and denominator are divisible by 3. So divide numerator and denominator by 3:

Numerator becomes √3, Denominator becomes 2.

- Identified b² =9 (under y²), so b=3.
- Since a > b, major axis is along the x-axis.
- Formula for e when a > b: e = sqrt(a² - b²)/a
- So c = sqrt(36 - 9) = sqrt(27) = 3√3.
- Then e = (3√3)/6 = √3/2.

The given equation of the ellipse is in standard form \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where \( a > b \). Here, \( a^2 = 36 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \), so \( a = 6 \) and \( b = 3 \).

The eccentricity \( e \) of an ellipse is calculated using the formula:
\[ e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a} \]

Substituting the known values:
\[ c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} = \sqrt{36 - 9} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3} \]
\[ e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \]

Thus, the eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

**Answer:**

First, let me recall what an ellipse is. An ellipse is a set of points where the sum of the distances from any point on the ellipse to the two foci is constant. That constant is equal to the length of the major axis. Wait, actually, isn't it twice the semi-major axis? Yeah, so if the major axis is 100 units long, then the semi-major axis 'a' must be half of that. So a = 50.

Now, the foci are located along the major axis. Since the foci are at (40,0) and (-40,0), they lie on the x-axis because their y-coordinates are zero. That means the major axis is along the x-axis. Therefore, the standard equation of the ellipse will be in the form:

(x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1

Where 'a' is the semi-major axis, which we found to be 50, and 'b' is the semi-minor axis.

Next, I need to find 'b'. For an ellipse, there's a relationship between a, b, and c, where 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus. The formula is:

a² = b² + c²

So, first, let me figure out what 'c' is. Since the foci are at (40,0) and (-40,0), the center of the ellipse is the midpoint between these two points. The midpoint would be ((-40+40)/2, (0+0)/2) which simplifies to (0,0). So, the center is at the origin.

The distance from the center to each focus is 40 units because one focus is at (40,0), so c = 40.

Now, using the relationship:

a² = b² + c²

We can plug in the values we have:

50² = b² + 40²
2500 = b² + 1600

Subtracting 1600 from both sides:

2500 - 1600 = b²
900 = b²

So, b is the square root of 900, which is 30.

Now that I have a and b, I can plug them back into the standard equation. Since the major axis is along the x-axis, the x-term comes first.

Therefore, the equation is:

(x²/50²) + (y²/30²) = 1

Which simplifies to:

x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1

So that should be the equation of the ellipse.
</think>

The ellipse has its foci on the x-axis, with a major axis length of 100. This gives a semi-major axis \( a = 50 \). The distance from the center to each focus is \( c = 40 \). Using the relationship \( a^2 = b^2 + c^2 \), we find \( b = 30 \). Therefore, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{50^2} + \frac{y^2}{30^2} = 1
\]

Next, let's tackle the asymptotes. I recall that for a hyperbola of the form x²/a² - y²/b² = 1, the equations of the asymptotes are y = ±(b/a)x + k. Since our center is at (0,0), there’s no k to add, so it simplifies to y = ±(b/a)x.

c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

Substituting the known values:

\[
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2 \\
25 = 9 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 25 - 9 \\
b^2 = 16
\]

So \(b\) is 4, but since we need \(b^2\) for the equation, that's 16.

Now plugging \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) into the standard form of the hyperbola:

\[
\frac{y^2}{3^2} - \frac{x^2}{4^2} = 1 \\
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

The vertex is exactly halfway between the focus and the directrix. So if the vertex is at (0, 0) and the focus is at (0, 4), I can figure out where the directrix must be.

Since the focus is at (0, 4), which is above the vertex, the parabola opens upwards. That means the directrix will be a horizontal line below the vertex. The distance from the vertex to the focus is |4 - 0| = 4 units. So, the directrix should be 4 units on the opposite side of the vertex from the focus. Therefore, the directrix must be at y = -4.

Now, I need to find the equation of this parabola. The standard form of a vertical parabola (one that opens up or down) with vertex at (h, k) is given by:

\( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \)

Where:
- (h, k) is the vertex,
- p is the distance from the vertex to the focus (if p is positive, it opens upwards; if negative, downwards).

In this case, the vertex is at (0, 0), so h = 0 and k = 0. The focus is at (0, 4), which means p = 4 because that's how far the focus is from the vertex.

Substituting these values into the standard equation:

\( (x - 0)^2 = 4 * 4 * (y - 0) \)

Simplifying this, we get:

\( x^2 = 16y \)

Wait, let me double-check. The focus is at (0, 4), so p should be the distance from the vertex to the focus, which is indeed 4 units. So yes, plugging that into the equation gives \( x^2 = 16y \).

I can also think about it another way to verify. For a parabola opening upwards with vertex at (0,0), any point (x,y) on the parabola should be equidistant from the focus (0,4) and the directrix y=-4.

The distance from (x,y) to the focus (0,4) is \( \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 4)^2} \).

The distance from (x,y) to the directrix y = -4 is |y - (-4)| = |y + 4|.

Setting these equal:

\( \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 4)^2} = |y + 4| \)

Squaring both sides to eliminate the square root and absolute value:

\( x^2 + (y - 4)^2 = (y + 4)^2 \)

Expanding both sides:

Left side: \( x^2 + y^2 - 8y + 16 \)
Right side: \( y^2 + 8y + 16 \)

Subtracting the right side from both sides to bring everything to one side:

\( x^2 + y^2 - 8y + 16 - y^2 - 8y - 16 = 0 \)

Simplifying:

\( x^2 - 16y = 0 \)
Which rearranges to \( x^2 = 16y \).

Okay, that matches what I had earlier. So I'm confident the equation is correct.

I think that's it. The standard form works out and when I did the distance check, both methods led me to the same equation.

To find the **vertex form** of the quadratic equation:

\[ y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify Coefficients
The general form of a quadratic equation is \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \). Here, 
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)
- \( c = 5 \)

### Step 2: Find the Vertex Coordinates

The vertex of a parabola given by \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \) is at the point \( (h, k) \), where:
\[ h = -\frac{b}{2a} \]
\[ k = c - \frac{b^2}{4a} \]

**Calculating \( h \):**
\[ h = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -1 \]

**Calculating \( k \):**
\[ k = 5 - \frac{6^2}{4 \times 3} = 5 - \frac{36}{12} = 5 - 3 = 2 \]

### Step 3: Write the Vertex Form

The vertex form of a quadratic equation is:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]

Plugging in the values we found:
\[ y = 3(x - (-1))^2 + 2 \]
\[ y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \]

### Final Answer

\boxed{y=3(x+1)^2+2}

First, subtract \( x^2 \), add \( 4x \), and subtract \( 12 \) from both sides:
\[
8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12
\]

Next, divide every term by 8 to solve for y:
\[
y = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 12}{8}
\]
This simplifies to:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Now, I'll complete the square for the quadratic expression in x. Start by factoring out \( -\frac{1}{8} \) from the first two terms:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

To complete the square inside the parentheses, I take half of the coefficient of x, which is \( -2 \), and square it to get 4. Add this value inside the parentheses:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x + 4) - \frac{3}{2} + \frac{1}{2}
\]

This simplifies to:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

To convert the standard form equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) into vertex form, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Isolate the y Term
Start by moving all terms except those involving y to one side of the equation.

\[
8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12
\]

### Step 2: Solve for y
Divide every term by 8 to solve for y.

\[
y = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 12}{8}
\]

Simplify the equation:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square
To write in vertex form, complete the square for the quadratic expression in x.

Start with:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Complete the square inside the parentheses. Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -2 \), and square it to get 4.

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left[(x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4\right] - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Simplify:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Combine constants:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

### Final Answer:
The vertex form of the equation is:

\[
\boxed{y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1}
\]

The equation of the parabola that passes through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) is found by solving a system of equations derived from these points. The general quadratic equation y = ax² + bx + c is used, where substituting each point gives:

- For (0,4): c = 4
- For (2,8): 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → 4a + 2b = 4
- For (-1,3): 3 = a - b + 4 → a - b = -1

Solving this system yields a = 1/3 and b = 4/3. Therefore, the equation is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

To find the equation of the parabola, I'll start by noting that it has a vertical axis of symmetry since its vertex is at (2, -1) and the directrix is y = -4.

(y + 1)² = 4p(x - 2).

Solving for p gives:

To find the equation of the parabola with vertex \((2, -1)\) and directrix \(y = -4\), we follow these steps:

**Step 1: Determine the Direction of the Parabola**

Since the directrix is a horizontal line (\(y = -4\)), the parabola opens **upward** because the vertex lies above the directrix.

**Step 2: Use the Standard Form of a Vertical Parabola**

The standard form for a vertical parabola with vertex \((h, k)\) is:
\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]
Where:
- \((h, k)\) is the vertex.
- \(p\) is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix).

**Step 3: Plug in the Known Values**

Given that the vertex is \((2, -1)\), we have:
\[
(y + 1)^2 = 4p(x - 2)
\]

**Step 4: Determine \(p\) Using the Directrix**

The distance from the vertex to the directrix (\(d\)) is related to \(p\) by the formula:
\[
d = |k - d_{directrix}|
\]
In this case, since the parabola opens upward and the directrix is below the vertex, we have:
\[
-1 - (-4) = 3
\]
Therefore, \(p = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{3}{2}\). However, considering the standard form and typical conventions, \(p\) represents twice this distance. Thus, \(p = 6\).

**Step 5: Substitute \(p\) Back into the Equation**

Substituting \(p = 6\) into the equation:
\[
(y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 6(x - 2)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
(y + 1)^2 = 24(x - 2)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(y + 1)^2 = 24(x - 2)}
\]

To convert the given equation into the standard form of a circle:

1. Start with the general form: \(x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0\).
2. Group x terms and y terms:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]
3. Complete the square for both groups:
   - For \(x^2 - 4x\), add \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \).
   - For \(y^2 + 2y\), add \( (2/2)^2 = 1 \).
4. Add these squares to both sides:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]
5. Simplify:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

The standard form of the circle is:

\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16}

To find the equation of the circle with its center at \((-2, 5)\) and passing through the point \((1, 9)\), we follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Radius
The radius \(r\) of the circle is the distance between the center \((-2, 5)\) and the point \((1, 9)\). We use the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
r = d = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

### Step 2: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting the known values (\(h = -2\), \(k = 5\), and \(r = 5\)):
\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2 \\
\Rightarrow \boxed{(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25}
\]

### Final Answer:
The equation of the circle is \(\boxed{(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25}\).

To find the equation of a circle centered at the origin (0, 0) and passing through the point (0, 5), follow these steps:

1. **Recall the Standard Equation of a Circle:**
   
   The general equation for a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
   Since the center is at the origin, \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\), so the equation simplifies to:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]

2. **Determine the Radius (\(r\)):**
   
   The circle passes through the point (0, 5). Substitute this point into the equation to find \(r\):
   \[
   0^2 + 5^2 = r^2 \\
   0 + 25 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]
   
3. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   
   Now that we know \(r^2 = 25\), substitute this back into the simplified equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the circle is \boxed{x^2 + y^2 = 25}.

The line y = x + 3 intersects the circle x² + y² = 17 at two points.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Substitute the expression for y from the line into the circle equation:**
   Replace y in x² + y² = 17 with (x + 3):
   \[
   x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
   \]

2. **Expand and simplify the equation:**
   Expand (x + 3)² to get:
   \[
   x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
   \]
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \\
   2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
   \]

3. **Simplify the quadratic equation:**
   Divide all terms by 2:
   \[
   x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
   \]

4. **Factor the quadratic equation:**
   Find two numbers that multiply to -4 and add to 3, which are 4 and -1.
   Thus,
   \[
   (x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
   \]
   
5. **Solve for x:**
   Set each factor equal to zero:
   \[
   x + 4 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -4 \\
   x - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 1
   \]

6. **Find corresponding y values using the line equation:**
   For x = -4:
   \[
   y = (-4) + 3 = -1
   \]
   So, one point is (-4, -1).

   For x = 1:
   \[
   y = (1) + 3 = 4
   \]
   The other point is (1, 4).

7. **Verify the solutions:**
   Ensure both points satisfy the original equations.

**Final Answer:**
The points of intersection are (-4, -1) and (1, 4).

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a circle that passes through three given points: (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1). Hmm, how do I approach this?

So, plugging in each point into the general equation gives me a system of equations:

1. For (1, 1): \((1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2\)
2. For (2, -3): \((2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2\)
3. For (5, -1): \((5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2\)

Now I have three equations:

1. \((1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2\)  -- Let's call this Equation A
2. \((2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2\) -- Equation B
3. \((5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2\) -- Equation C

Since all equal to \(r^2\), I can set them equal to each other. Let's subtract Equation A from Equation B first.

Equation B minus Equation A:

\[
[(2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2] - [(1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2] = 0
\]

Expanding both squares:

First term: \((2 - h)^2 = 4 - 4h + h^2\)
Second term: \((-3 - k)^2 = 9 + 6k + k^2\)
Third term: \((1 - h)^2 = 1 - 2h + h^2\)
Fourth term: \((1 - k)^2 = 1 - 2k + k^2\)

Putting it all together:

[ (4 - 4h + h^2) + (9 + 6k + k^2) ] - [ (1 - 2h + h^2) + (1 - 2k + k^2) ] = 0

Simplify inside the brackets:

First bracket: 4 + 9 - 4h + 6k + h^2 + k^2 = 13 - 4h + 6k + h^2 + k^2
Second bracket: 1 + 1 - 2h - 2k + h^2 + k^2 = 2 - 2h - 2k + h^2 + k^2

Now subtract the second from the first:

(13 - 4h + 6k + h^2 + k^2) - (2 - 2h - 2k + h^2 + k^2) = 0

Simplify term by term:

13 - 2 = 11
-4h - (-2h) = -2h
6k - (-2k) = 8k
h^2 - h^2 cancels out
k^2 - k^2 cancels out

So overall: 11 - 2h + 8k = 0

Let's rearrange:

-2h + 8k = -11 --> divide both sides by 2 to simplify:
-h + 4k = -5.5 or h = 4k + 5.5 -- Let's call this Equation D.

Now, let's subtract Equation A from Equation C to get another equation.

Equation C minus Equation A:

\[
[(5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2] - [(1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2] = 0
\]

Expanding each term:

First: \((5 - h)^2 = 25 - 10h + h^2\)
Second: \((-1 - k)^2 = 1 + 2k + k^2\)
Third: \((1 - h)^2 = 1 - 2h + h^2\)
Fourth: \((1 - k)^2 = 1 - 2k + k^2\)

Putting it all together:

[ (25 - 10h + h^2) + (1 + 2k + k^2) ] - [ (1 - 2h + h^2) + (1 - 2k + k^2) ] = 0

Simplify inside the brackets:

First bracket: 25 + 1 -10h + 2k + h^2 + k^2 = 26 -10h + 2k + h^2 + k^2
Second bracket: 1 + 1 -2h -2k + h^2 + k^2 = 2 - 2h -2k + h^2 + k^2

Subtract the second from the first:

(26 -10h + 2k) - (2 - 2h -2k) = 0

Simplify term by term:

26 - 2 = 24
-10h - (-2h) = -8h
2k - (-2k) = 4k

So overall: 24 -8h +4k=0

Let's simplify this equation. Divide all terms by 4:

6 - 2h + k = 0 --> rearranged as -2h + k = -6 -- Let's call this Equation E.

Now we have two equations:

Equation D: h = 4k +5.5
Equation E: -2h +k =-6

Substitute h from Equation D into Equation E:

-2*(4k +5.5) +k = -6

Compute:

-8k -11 +k = -6 --> (-7k) -11 = -6

Add 11 to both sides: -7k = 5 --> k= -5/7 ≈ -0.714

Now plug this back into Equation D to find h.

h=4*(-5/7)+5.5 = -20/7 + 38.5/7 (since 5.5 is 38.5/7) Wait, maybe better as decimals:

-20/7 ≈ -2.857

So h≈ -2.857 +5.5= approximately 2.642?

Wait, let me do exact fractions.

h = (4*(-5/7)) +11/2

First compute 4*(-5/7)= -20/7

Convert 11/2 to sevenths: 11*7 /14? Wait, no:

To add -20/7 and 11/2, find a common denominator. 14.

-20/7 = (-40)/14
11/2= (77)/14

So h=(-40 +77)/14=37/14≈2.642857

Similarly, k=-5/7

Now we have the center at (h,k)=(37/14, -5/7)

Next, compute radius r.

Use one of the original equations, say Equation A:

(1 - h)^2 + (1 -k)^2 =r^2

1 -k:1 -(-5/7)=1+5/7=12/7≈1.7143

Now square them:

(-23/14)^2= (529)/(196)
(12/7)^2= 144/(49) = 576/196

So total: (529 + 576)/196= 1105/196=r^2

Thus, r= sqrt(1105/196)=sqrt(1105)/14

Wait, is 1105 divisible by any square? Let's factorize.

1105 divided by 5: 221. Then 221 is 13*17. So sqrt(1105) doesn't simplify further.

So the equation is (x -37/14)^2 + (y +5/7)^2 = (sqrt(1105)/14)^2, which is 1105/196.

Alternatively, to write it neatly:

\[

\]

Grouping like terms:

Completing the square for each group:

For x: (x - 4)² - 16
For y: (y - 3)² - 9

Substituting back:
(x - 4)² - 16 + (y - 3)² - 9 + 20 = 0

Simplify constants:
-16 - 9 + 20 = -5

Equation becomes:
(x - 4)² + (y - 3)² = 5

The problem requires finding the equation of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis length of 10 and a minor axis length of 6.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the standard form:** For an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis, the equation is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   where \(a\) is the semi-major axis and \(b\) is the semi-minor axis.

2. **Determine \(a\):** The length of the major axis is 10, so half of that (the semi-major axis) is:
   \[
   a = \frac{10}{2} = 5
   \]

3. **Determine \(b\):** The length of the minor axis is 6, so half of that (the semi-minor axis) is:
   \[
   b = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]

4. **Substitute into the equation:**
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25} + \dfrac{y^2}{9} = 1}\).

**Given Equation:**
\[ 9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36 \]

**Step 1: Divide both sides by 36 to normalize the equation.**

\[
\frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]

Simplify each term:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

**Step 2: Identify the standard form of an ellipse.**

The standard form of an ellipse is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

We can see that:
\[
a^2 = 4 \quad \text{and} \quad b^2 = 9
\]
Thus,

a = 2 \quad \text{and} \quad b = 3

***********************<think>
First, I need to determine the standard form equation of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis.

The general equation for such an ellipse is x²/a² + y²/b² = 1, where 'a' is the semi-major axis and 'b' is the semi-minor axis.

Given that the length of the major axis is 100, the semi-major axis 'a' is half of that, so a = 50.

The foci are located at (±30, 0), which means c = 30. For an ellipse, the relationship between a, b, and c is given by c² = a² - b².

Substituting the known values into this equation:
30² = 50² - b²
900 = 2500 - b²

Solving for b² gives:
b² = 2500 - 900 = 1600
Therefore, b = √1600 = 40.

Now, plugging a and b back into the standard equation of the ellipse:

x²/50² + y²/40² = 1

Simplifying further:
x²/2500 + y²/1600 = 1

To find the equation of an ellipse with the given parameters, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**
   
   The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis is:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
   where \( a \) is the length of the semi-major axis and \( b \) is the length of the semi-minor axis.

2. **Determine the Semi-Major Axis (\( a \)):**
   
   The major axis has a length of 100, so the semi-major axis is half of that:
   
   \[
   a = \frac{100}{2} = 50
   \]
   
3. **Determine the Distance to Foci (\( c \)):**
   
   The foci are located at (±30, 0), so \( c = 30 \).

4. **Find the Semi-Minor Axis (\( b \)):**
   
   For an ellipse, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) is given by:
   
   \[
   c^2 = a^2 - b^2
   \]
   
   Plugging in the known values:
   
   \[
   30^2 = 50^2 - b^2 \\
   900 = 2500 - b^2 \\
   b^2 = 2500 - 900 = 1600 \\
   b = \sqrt{1600} = 40
   \]
   
5. **Write the Equation of the Ellipse:**
   
   Substitute \( a \) and \( b \) back into the standard form:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{50^2} + \frac{y^2}{40^2} = 1 \\
   \frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^{2}}{2500}+\dfrac{y^{2}}{1600}=1}
\]

The given problem involves determining the equation of an ellipse based on its vertices and foci. 

1. **Identify the type of ellipse**: Since the vertices and foci lie along the y-axis, this is a vertical ellipse with major axis along the y-axis.

2. **Determine key parameters**:
   - The vertices are at (0, 7) and (0, -7), so 'a' (semi-major axis length) is 7.
     Thus, \( a^2 = 49 \).
   - The foci are at (0, 5) and (0, -5), so 'c' (distance from center to focus) is 5.

3. **Use the relationship between a, b, and c**:
   For an ellipse: \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \)
   Solving for \( b^2 \):
   \[ 25 = 49 - b^2 \]
   \[ b^2 = 49 - 25 = 24 \]

4. **Formulate the equation**:
   Substituting into the standard form of a vertical ellipse:
   \[ \frac{y^2}{49} + \frac{x^2}{24} = 1 \]

**Final Answer**: The equation is \(\frac{y^2}{49} + \frac{x^2}{24} = 1\).

The real solutions to the equation occur at specific points:

- When \( h = 0 \), any value of \( k \) satisfies the equation because both sides reduce appropriately.
- When \( |h| = 1 \), substituting \( h = 1 \) or \( h = -1 \) into the equation yields valid solutions for particular \( k \) values.

Thus, the real number pairs that satisfy the equation are:

\[
(h, k) = (0, k), \quad (h, k) = (1, 2), \quad (h, k) = (-1, 0)
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Equation Setup:** Start with the given equation involving real numbers \( h \) and \( k \).

2. **Consider Zero Cases:** If \( h = 0 \), substitute into the equation to see if it holds true for any \( k \). It simplifies correctly, so all pairs where \( h=0 \) are solutions.

3. **Non-Zero \( h \):** Assume \( h \neq 0 \), then manipulate the equation to express in terms of another variable or find relations between \( h \) and \( k \).

4. **Substitution for Simplification:** Use substitution methods, such as letting \( u = h^2 \) or considering symmetry where \( h = k \), to simplify the equation.

5. **Polynomial Expansion:** Expand any polynomial expressions resulting from substitutions to equate coefficients with terms on both sides of the equation.

6. **Testing Possible Values:** Test specific integer values (e.g., \( h=1, -1, 2 \)) and solve for corresponding \( k \) values analytically or numerically.

7. **Check for Real Roots:** Determine if higher-degree polynomial equations have real solutions by testing possible rational roots or analyzing the behavior of the function.

8. **Synthesis of Solutions:** Compile all valid pairs (\( h, k \)) found through these methods to form the complete set of real number solutions.

**Final Answer:**

The area of an ellipse can be calculated using the formula \( A = \pi \times a \times b \), where \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

Given the equation of the ellipse:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

We can identify:
- \( a^2 = 4 \), so \( a = 2 \)
- \( b^2 = 9 \), so \( b = 3 \)

Substituting these into the area formula:

\[
A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi
\]

\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Given the center is (-2, 1), so substitute \( h = -2 \) and \( k = 1 \):

\frac{(x + 2)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

2b = 8 \\

To find the equation of an ellipse given its center, horizontal major axis length, and vertical minor axis length, follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Understand the Standard Form of an Ellipse**

The standard form of an ellipse centered at \((h, k)\) is:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Where:
- \( (h, k) \) is the center of the ellipse.
- \( a \) is the semi-major axis length (\(a > b\) for a horizontal major axis).
- \( b \) is the semi-minor axis length.

### **Step 2: Identify Given Values**

From the problem:
- Center of the ellipse, \( (h, k) = (-2, 1) \)
- Length of the **horizontal major axis** = 12
- Length of the **vertical minor axis** = 8

### **Step 3: Calculate Semi-Major and Semi-Minor Axes**

Since the major axis is horizontal:
\[
a = \frac{\text{Length of Major Axis}}{2} = \frac{12}{2} = 6
\]
And since the minor axis is vertical:
\[
b = \frac{\text{Length of Minor Axis}}{2} = \frac{8}{2} = 4
\]

### **Step 4: Plug Values into the Standard Form**

Substitute \( h = -2 \), \( k = 1 \), \( a^2 = 36 \), and \( b^2 = 16 \) into the standard equation:
\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
\]

### **Final Answer**

The equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(x+2)^2}{36}+\dfrac{(y-1)^2}{16}=1}
\]

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
\Rightarrow \frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

$$
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
$$

\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
$$

$$

$$

$$

Multiplying both sides by 5 gives b = 3.

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a parabola. The given information is that the vertex is at (0, 0) and the focus is at (0, 4). Hmm, let me think about how to approach this.

First, I remember that the standard form of a parabola depends on whether it opens up, down, left, or right. Since both the vertex and focus have the same x-coordinate (which is 0), this means the parabola must open either upwards or downwards. The y-coordinates differ: the vertex is at (0,0) and the focus is at (0,4). Since 4 is positive, it's above the vertex, so the parabola opens upward.

Now, for a parabola that opens up or down, the standard form is \( y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \), where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus. In this case, since the focus is at (0,4), the value of p should be 4 because it's four units away from the vertex.

So plugging p into the equation: \( y = \frac{1}{4*4}x^2 \) which simplifies to \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

Wait, is that right? Let me double-check. The formula for a parabola opening upwards with vertex at (h,k) is usually given as \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \). Since the vertex is at (0,0), it simplifies to \( x^2 = 4py \). Then solving for y gives \( y = \frac{x^2}{4p} \).

Yes, so p here is 4. So plugging in, we get \( y = \frac{x^2}{16} \) or \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \). That seems consistent.

I think that's the correct equation.
</think>

The parabola opens upwards with vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). Using the standard form \( x^2 = 4py \), where p is 4, we find the equation to be \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the direction of the parabola:** Since the focus (0,4) is above the vertex (0,0), it opens upwards.

2. **Use the standard form for an upward opening parabola:** \( x^2 = 4py \).

3. **Determine p from the distance between vertex and focus:** The distance from (0,0) to (0,4) is 4 units, so p = 4.

4. **Substitute p into the standard equation:** \( x^2 = 16y \).

5. **Solve for y to get the final form:** Divide both sides by 16 to obtain \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

$$(x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)$$

$$(x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4)$$

The vertex form of a quadratic equation is useful because it directly provides the coordinates of the vertex (h, k). For the given equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we can convert it to vertex form by completing the square.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Start with the original equation:**
   \[
   y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 2/2 = 1 \).
   - Square this value to get \( 1 \).

4. **Add and subtract this squared value inside the parentheses:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5
   \]
   This step effectively adds zero (since we're adding and then subtracting the same value), but it allows us to form a perfect square trinomial.

5. **Rewrite the expression as a perfect square:**
   \[
   y = 3[(x + 1)^2 - 1] + 5
   \]

6. **Distribute the factor of 3 and simplify:**
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

So, the vertex form of the equation is:
\[
y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]

The final answer in JSON format is:

The equation of the parabola can be found using three points by setting up a system of equations based on the general quadratic form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \). Plugging in each point gives:

1. For (0,4): \( 4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \Rightarrow c = 4 \).
2. For (2,8): \( 8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \Rightarrow 4a + 2b = 4 \).
3. For (-1,3): \( 3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \Rightarrow a - b = -1 \).

Solving the system of equations:
- From the third equation: \( a = b - 1 \).
- Substituting into the second equation: \( 4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \Rightarrow 6b = 8 \Rightarrow b = \frac{4}{3} \).

Given that the vertex is at \( (0, 0) \), the focus is:

Next, using the midpoint as the center (h, k), I'll calculate the radius by finding the distance from the midpoint to one of the endpoints.

To determine the equation of a circle with endpoints of its diameter at \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the Center of the Circle**

The center \((h, k)\) is the midpoint between the two endpoints. Use the midpoint formula:
\[
h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is \((2, -1)\).

**Step 2: Calculate the Radius**

The radius \(r\) is half the distance between the two endpoints. First, find the distance \(d\) using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
Then, the radius is:
\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

**Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle**

The standard form of the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting the values found:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplifying:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(x-2)^2+(y+1)^2=20}
\]

To find the center and radius of the circle defined by the equation \( x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Rewrite the Equation**

Start with the given equation:
\[
x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \) and \( y \)**

Group the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together:
\[
(x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
\]

Complete the square for each group.

For the \( x \)-terms:
\[
x^2 + 6x = (x + 3)^2 - 9
\]

For the \( y \)-terms:
\[
y^2 - 4y = (y - 2)^2 - 4
\]

**Step 3: Substitute Back into the Equation**

Substitute the completed squares back into the equation:
\[
(x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
\]

Combine constants on the right side:
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12
\]
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Step 4: Identify the Center and Radius**

The equation is now in standard form:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \( (h, k) \) is the center and \( r \) is the radius.

Comparing with our equation:
\[
h = -3,\quad k = 2,\quad r^2 = 25
\]

So,
\[
\text{Center} = (-3, 2)
\]
\[
\text{Radius} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

**Final Answer:**
The center of the circle is \( (-3, 2) \) and its radius is \( 5 \).

     \]
   
3. **Substitute \(r^2\) back into the general equation**:
   - Plugging \(25\) for \(r^2\) gives:
     \[
     x^2 + y^2 = 25
     \]

4. **Final Answer**:
   \[
   \boxed{x^{2}+y^{2}=25}
   \]

$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$

where $(h, k)$ is the center of the circle and $r$ is its radius.

Wait maybe let me explain more systematically:

Given three points on a circle: A(x1,y1), B(x2,y2), C(x3,y3). The standard method is to plug each point into the general equation of the circle, which is x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0. Then you have three equations:

For point A: x1² + y1² + D*x1 + E*y1 + F = 0

Similarly for B and C.

Solving these three linear equations will give values of D, E, F, which can then be used to find the center (h,k) as (-D/2, -E/2), and radius r is sqrt( (D/2)^2 + (E/2)^2 - F )

Alternatively, if you have the three points in terms of coordinates, say A(a,b), B(c,d), C(e,f), then setting up those equations will allow solving for D, E, F.

But if I were to write a general method:

1. For each given point (x,y), plug into x² + y² +Dx +Ey +F=0.

Each gives an equation: for n points, get system of equations.

2. Solve this linear system for D,E,F.

3. Once have D,E,F, the circle's center is (-D/2, -E/2), radius sqrt( (D/2)^2 + (E/2)^2 -F ).

Alternatively, in standard form after completing squares as above.

So going back to my original problem: if I have three points, solve the system for D,E,F. Then convert to center-radius form.

But since the actual given points were arbitrary, let's see how that process works in another way—using perpendicular bisectors.

Alternatively, method using midpoint and slope:

Given two points on the circle, their perpendicular bisector will pass through the center.

Similarly, with three non-colinear points, I can find the intersection of two perpendicular bisectors—their intersection is the center. Then compute radius as distance from center to any point.

So steps:

1. Find midpoints between each pair of given points.

2. For each midpoint, calculate slope of the segment. The perpendicular bisector will have slope negative reciprocal.

3. Write equations for the two perpendicular bisectors using these slopes and midpoints.

4. Solve these two equations—intersection is center (h,k).

5. Compute radius as distance from (h,k) to any given point on circle.

6. Then, if needed, write equation in standard form: (x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2.

So let me try this approach with the example points:

A(0,-8), B(-2,6), C(4,12).

First step, find midpoints of AB and AC.

Midpoint of AB:

x=(0-2)/2= -1; y=(-8 +6)/2 = (-2)/2=-1. So midpoint M1: (-1,-1)

Slope of AB: (6 - (-8))/(-2 -0)=14/-2= -7.

Thus, perpendicular bisector slope is reciprocal positive: 1/7

Equation of perpendicular bisector through M1:

y - (-1) = (1/7)(x - (-1)) --> y +1 = (1/7)(x +1)

Simplify to standard form:

Multiply both sides by 7: 7(y+1)=x+1 => x -7y=6

So equation 1: x -7y=6.

Now, midpoint of AC: points A(0,-8), C(4,12).

Midpoint M2: ( (0 +4)/2, (-8 +12)/2 ) = (2,2)

Slope of AC: (12 -(-8))/(4-0)=20/4=5.

Perpendicular bisector slope is -1/5

Equation of perpendicular bisector through M2(2,2):

y - 2 = (-1/5)(x -2 )

Multiply both sides by 5:

5(y -2) = -(x -2)

Simplify: 5y -10= -x +2 => x +5y =12

So equation 2: x +5y=12.

Now, solve the two equations:

Equation1: x -7y=6

Equation2: x +5y=12

Subtract Equation1 from Equation2:

(x +5y) - (x -7y)=12 -6 => 12y =6 => y=0.5.

Then, substitute back into equation1: x -7*(0.5)=6 -->x -3.5=6-->x=9.5

So center at (9.5, 0.5)

h=-D/2; k=-E/2.

(x-9)^2 + (y -3/7)^2 = ... Wait, no—it was center at (9, 3/7). But wait: here via perpendicular bisectors I get (9.5,0.5), which is same as (19/2, 1/2).

To solve this problem, we need to determine the equation of a circle that passes through three given non-colinear points in a 2D plane. The goal is to find the center and radius of this circle.

### Approach
1. **Equation Setup**: The general equation of a circle passing through three points \((x_1, y_1)\), \((x_2, y_2)\), and \((x_3, y_3)\) can be written as:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0
   \]
   where \(D\), \(E\), and \(F\) are constants to be determined.

2. **Formulating Linear Equations**: By substituting the three points into the general equation, we derive a system of linear equations in terms of \(D\), \(E\), and \(F\):
   - For point \((x_1, y_1)\): \(x_1^2 + y_1^2 + Dx_1 + Ey_1 + F = 0\)
   - For point \((x_2, y_2)\): \(x_2^2 + y_2^2 + Dx_2 + Ey_2 + F = 0\)
   - For point \((x_3, y_3)\): \(x_3^2 + y_3^2 + Dx_3 + Ey_3 + F = 0\)

3. **Matrix Formulation**: Subtracting pairs of these equations eliminates \(F\) and results in a system of two linear equations with two variables (\(D\) and \(E\)):
   - Using points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\): \(A_1 D + B_1 E = C_1\)
   - Using points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_3, y_3)\): \(A_2 D + B_2 E = C_2\)

4. **Solving the Linear System**: The coefficients \(A_1, B_1, C_1\) and \(A_2, B_2, C_2\) are computed from the coordinates of the points. We solve this system using matrix operations to find \(D\) and \(E\).

5. **Finding \(F\)**: Once \(D\) and \(E\) are known, substitute back into one of the original equations to find \(F\).

6. **Circle Properties**: The center \((h, k)\) is given by \((-D/2, -E/2)\), and the radius \(r\) is computed as:
   \[
   r = \sqrt{\left(\frac{D^2 + E^2}{4} - F\right)}
   \]

### Solution Code
```python
def circle_through_three_points(p1, p2, p3):
    x1, y1 = p1
    x2, y2 = p2
    x3, y3 = p3

    # Compute A1, B1, C1 from equation involving points p1 and p2
    A1 = x1 - x2
    B1 = y1 - y2
    C1 = -( (x1**2 + y1**2) - (x2**2 + y2**2) )

    # Compute A2, B2, C2 from equation involving points p1 and p3
    A2 = x1 - x3
    B2 = y1 - y3
    C2 = -( (x1**2 + y1**2) - (x3**2 + y3**2) )

    # Solve the system of equations:
    # A1*D + B1*E = C1
    # A2*D + B2*E = C2

    denominator = A1 * B2 - A2 * B1
    if denominator == 0:
        return None  # Not possible with these points (shouldn't happen for non-colinear distinct points)

    D = (B2 * C1 - B1 * C2) / denominator
    E = (A1 * C2 - A2 * C1) / denominator

    F = -(x1**2 + y1**2 + D*x1 + E*y1)

    # Compute the center and radius of the circle
    h, k = -D/2.0, -E/2.0
    r_squared = (h**2 + k**2) - F
    if r_squared < 0:
        return None  # No real solution; points might be colinear or invalid
    radius = math.sqrt(r_squared)

    return (center=(h, k), radius=radius)

# Example usage:
import math

p1 = (0, 0)
p2 = (1, 0)
p3 = (0, 1)

circle = circle_through_three_points(p1, p2, p3)
print("Circle center:", circle.center)
print("Circle radius:", circle.radius)
```

### Explanation
- **Equation Setup**: The general equation of a circle is used to form linear equations based on given points.
- **Linear System**: By subtracting pairs of these equations, we eliminate \(F\) and solve for \(D\) and \(E\).
- **Matrix Operations**: Using matrix operations, we determine the values of \(D\), \(E\), and subsequently \(F\).
- **Circle Properties**: The center is derived from \(D\) and \(E\), while the radius is computed using these coefficients along with \(F\).

This approach efficiently finds the circle passing through three non-colinear points, ensuring accurate determination of its center and radius.

To find the center and radius of the circle given by the equation \(x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0\), we need to rewrite it in standard form. 

First, we complete the square for both \(x\) and \(y\) terms.

1. Rewrite the given equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
   \]

2. Group the \(x\) terms and \(y\) terms:
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) + 20 = 0
   \]

3. Complete the square for \(x\):
   - Take the coefficient of \(x\), which is \(-8\), divide by 2 to get \(-4\), and square it to get \(16\).
   - Add and subtract 16 inside the equation:
     \[
     (x^2 - 8x + 16 - 16)
     \]

4. Complete the square for \(y\):
   - Take the coefficient of \(y\), which is \(-6\), divide by 2 to get \(-3\), and square it to get \(9\).
   - Add and subtract 9 inside the equation:
     \[
     (y^2 - 6y + 9 - 9)
     \]

5. Rewrite the equation with completed squares:
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x + 16) - 16 + (y^2 - 6y + 9) - 9 + 20 = 0
   \]

6. Simplify the constants:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 - 16 - 9 + 20 = 0
   \]
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 - 5 = 0
   \]

7. Move the constant to the other side:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

From this, we can see that the equation is in standard form \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where the center \((h, k)\) is \((4, 3)\) and the radius \(r\) is \(\sqrt{5}\).

Thus, the coordinates of the center are:
\[
\boxed{(4, 3)}
\]

To determine the equation of a circle with its center at \((1, 2)\) and passing through the point \((4, 6)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Recall the General Equation of a Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 2: Identify the Given Information
- **Center (\(h, k\))**: \((1, 2)\)
- **Passing through a Point (x, y)**: \((4, 6)\)

### Step 3: Plug in the Center Coordinates into the Equation
Substitute \(h = 1\) and \(k = 2\) into the general equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

### Step 4: Find the Radius (\(r\)) Using the Given Point
The circle passes through \((4, 6)\). Substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\) into the equation to find \(r\):
\[
(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2 \\
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
9 + 16 = r^2 \\
25 = r^2
\]
Thus, \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\).

### Step 5: Write the Complete Equation of the Circle
Substitute \(h = 1\), \(k = 2\), and \(r = 5\) into the general equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2 \\
\Rightarrow \boxed{(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25}
\]

The equation of the ellipse with center at (0, 0), a horizontal major axis of length 10, and a minor axis of length 6 is derived as follows:

- Center: (0, 0)
- Major axis length = 10 ⇒ semi-major axis \(a = \frac{10}{2} = 5\)
- Minor axis length = 6 ⇒ semi-minor axis \(b = \frac{6}{2} = 3\)

The standard form of the ellipse equation is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Substituting the values of \(a\) and \(b\):
\[
\frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25}+\dfrac{y^2}{9}=1}\).

The given equation \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\) can be converted into the standard form of an ellipse by dividing both sides by 36, resulting in \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:** \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\).
2. **Divide every term by 36** to simplify and normalize the equation:
   - \(\frac{9x^2}{36} + \(\frac{4y^2}{36}\) = \(\frac{36}{36}\)
3. **Simplify each fraction:**
   - \(9/36 = 1/4\), so the first term becomes \(\frac{x^2}{4}\).
   - \(4/36 = 1/9\), so the second term becomes \(\frac{y^2}{9}\).
   - The right side simplifies to 1.
4. **Write the equation in standard form:** 
   - \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

This is now in the standard form of an ellipse.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**
1. **Identify the standard form:** The equation \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) is in standard form for an ellipse centered at (0,0).
2. **Determine a and b:** Here, a² = 36 so a = 6; b² = 9 so b = 3.
3. **Apply the eccentricity formula:** Since a > b, use \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

Now, I remember there's a formula for the area of an ellipse. It’s similar to the area of a circle but adjusted because it's stretched into an oval shape. The area should be \( \pi \times a \times b \), where a and b are the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

Let me try that quickly:

Parametrize y as a function of x: \( y = \frac{3}{2} \sqrt{4 - x^2} \). Then, integrating this from 0 to 2 would give the area in the first quadrant. Multiply by 4 for all quadrants.

So the integral is \( 4 \times \int_{0}^{2} \frac{3}{2} \sqrt{4 - x^2} dx \).

Let me compute that:

First, factor out constants: 4 * (3/2) = 6. So now it's 6 times the integral of sqrt(4 - x²) from 0 to 2.

The integral of sqrt(a² - x²) dx is known; it’s \( \frac{x}{2} \sqrt{a^2 - x^2} + \frac{a^2}{2} \arcsin(\frac{x}{a}) ) \).

In this case, a = 2. So plugging in:

From 0 to 2: [ (x/2)*sqrt(4 - x²) + (4/2)* arcsin(x/2) ] evaluated from 0 to 2.

At x=2: First term is (2/2)*sqrt(4-4)=1*0=0. Second term is 2 * arcsin(1)= 2*(pi/2) = pi.

At x=0: First term is 0, second term is 2 * arcsin(0)=0.

So the integral from 0 to 2 is (0 + pi) - (0 + 0) = pi. Therefore, multiplying by 6 gives 6*pi.

Yes! So both methods confirm that the area is \( 6\pi \).

From the given equation, we identify that \( a^2 = 4 \) (so \( a = 2 \)) and \( b^2 = 9 \) (so \( b = 3 \)). Plugging these into the area formula gives:

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a hyperbola given its foci and a point it passes through. Let me break this down step by step.

First, I remember that a hyperbola is defined as the set of all points where the absolute difference of the distances from two fixed points (the foci) is constant. The standard form of a hyperbola depends on whether it opens vertically or horizontally. Since the foci are given as (0, ±√10), they lie along the y-axis, which means this is a vertical hyperbola.

For a vertical hyperbola centered at the origin, the standard equation is:
\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Here, \( a \) and \( b \) are the distances related to the vertices and co-vertices, respectively. The distance between the center and each focus is denoted by \( c \), so in this case, \( c = \sqrt{10} \).

I also recall the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) for hyperbolas:
\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]
So, if I can find \( a^2 \), I can then find \( b^2 \) using this equation.

Next, the hyperbola passes through the point (2, 3). This means that when I plug x=2 and y=3 into the standard equation, it should satisfy the equation. Let me write that out:
\[
\frac{3^2}{a^2} - \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Which simplifies to:
\[
\frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]
Now, I have two equations involving \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \):
1. \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \) → \( 10 = a^2 + b^2 \)
2. \( \frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \)

I can solve these equations simultaneously to find the values of \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \). Let me denote \( A = a^2 \) and \( B = b^2 \) for simplicity. Then, the equations become:
1. \( A + B = 10 \)
2. \( \frac{9}{A} - \frac{4}{B} = 1 \)

From equation 1, I can express \( B = 10 - A \). Substitute this into equation 2:
\[
\frac{9}{A} - \frac{4}{10 - A} = 1
\]
To solve for \( A \), let's find a common denominator and combine the fractions. Multiply both sides by \( A(10 - A) \):
\[
9(10 - A) - 4A = A(10 - A)
\]
Expanding each term:
- Left side: \( 90 - 9A - 4A = 90 - 13A \)
- Right side: \( 10A - A^2 \)

So, bringing all terms to one side:
\[
90 - 13A = 10A - A^2
\]
Rearranging gives a quadratic equation:
\[
A^2 - 23A + 90 = 0
\]
Let me solve this quadratic. The discriminant \( D \) is \( (-23)^2 - 4(1)(90) = 529 - 360 = 169 \). So, the roots are:
\[
A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{169}}{2} = \frac{23 \pm 13}{2}
\]
Which gives two solutions:
- \( A = \frac{23 + 13}{2} = 18 \)
- \( A = \frac{23 - 13}{2} = 5 \)

So, \( a^2 = 18 \) or \( a^2 = 5 \). Then, using equation 1, if \( A=18 \), then \( B=10 - 18 = -8 \), which doesn't make sense because \( b^2 \) can't be negative. Therefore, this solution is invalid.

If \( A=5 \), then \( B=10 - 5 = 5 \). So both \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \) are 5? Wait, let me double-check that because substituting back into equation 2 might help verify.

Wait, if \( A=5 \), \( B=5 \), then plugging into equation 2:
\[
\frac{9}{5} - \frac{4}{5} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]
Which is correct. So both are valid in this context.

Therefore, the standard form of the hyperbola is:
\[
\frac{y^2}{5} - \frac{x^2}{5} = 1
\]
Alternatively, since \( a^2 = b^2 = 5 \), it simplifies to:
\[
\frac{y^2 - x^2}{5} = 1
\]
But usually, we keep the denominators separate for clarity.

The hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis and asymptotes of slopes ±2 can be written in standard form by using the relationship between a and b. Given that the slopes are ±2, we know \( \frac{b}{a} = 2 \), so \( b = 2a \). Assuming the hyperbola is centered at the origin for simplicity, the equation becomes:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{(2a)^2} = 1
\]

Choosing \( a = 1 \) simplifies this to:

\[
x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

This equation satisfies all the given conditions.

Final Answer: The equation of the hyperbola is \(\boxed{x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1}\).

The foci are at (0, ±5), so c is 5. Therefore, c² is 25.

The equation of a vertical hyperbola with vertices at (0, ±3) and foci at (0, ±5) can be determined using the standard form:

- The distance from the center to the vertices is \(a = 3\), so \(a^2 = 9\).
- The distance from the center to the foci is \(c = 5\), so \(c^2 = 25\).
- Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\), we solve for \(b^2 = c^2 - a^2 = 25 - 9 = 16\).

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]
